<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
OK so the stochastic dominance data file shows:
- Total unordered pairs: 105 ✓
- Dominant pairs: 61, non-dominant: 44
- dominant_pct: 58.1% ✓ (paper says 58%)

But the tier breakdown doesn't match what the paper says:
- Paper says "within-tier 72.1% non-dominant (31/43), cross-tier 21.0% non-dominant (13/62)"
- Data says "within_tier: total_pairs=31, non_dominant=21, non_dominant_pct=67.7%"


- Cross-tier shows total_pairs=74, non_dominant=23, non_dominant_pct=31.1%

The discrepancy is significant—the paper's tier counts (43 within-tier, 62 cross-tier) don't match the data (31 within-tier, 74 cross-tier), so either the paper used a different tier classification or there's an issue with how the tiers were defined in the data file.

Looking at the tier breakdown in the data, I can verify the pair counts: frontier has 4 models, mid has 6, and lightweight has 5. Computing the combinations—6 + 15 + 10 within-tier pairs and 24 + 20 + 30 cross-tier pairs—confirms the data's totals of 31 and 74 respectively.

However, the paper's Table 2 uses a completely different tier structure: frontier stays at 4 models, but mid-tier shrinks to just 2 (gpt-4.1 and claude-sonnet), while lightweight expands to 9 models. This discrepancy means the pair counts would shift significantly—43 within-tier pairs and a different cross-tier calculation. to be redone with the paper's tier classification. The overall 58% dominance rate holds regardless of tiers, but I need to recalculate the within-tier and cross-tier breakdowns using the paper's grouping instead of the data file's.

Actually, the dominance pairs themselves don't change—only how they're categorized by tier. So the 61 non-dominant pairs out of 105 total is still correct, but the 31/43 and 13/62 splits need to be recomputed. The problem is I don't have the individual pair-level dominance data broken down by the paper's tier assignments, only by the data file's original tiers. Recalculating this precisely would require going back to the raw pair data, which I don't have readily available.

I'll flag this as a discrepancy for verification rather than spend more time on it. The core audit findings are solid, so I should focus on ensuring the critical fixes are properly documented.
```

[Reviewer 回复]:
[severity: critical] Position Bias ASR 计算 bug 修复 — 需验证主实验管线是否同样受影响

## 发现

fig4 restyle-v2 worker 发现并修复了 **position bias ASR 的反转 bug**：

- `apply_position_attack` 只对 gt=A 的 pair 做 swap（swapped_gt 恒为 B）
- 旧代码对所有 position_bias scores 做 A↔B inversion，导致 gt=B 的 case 被错误反转
- **修复前**：顶级 API 模型 Position Bias ASR = 43-55%（虚高）
- **修复后**：顶级 API 模型 Position Bias ASR = 0-3%（合理）

这解释了为什么之前 radar chart 和 ASR violin plot 中 Position Bias 看起来是"最强攻击"——那是 bug 导致的假象。

## 关键问题：主实验管线是否也有这个 bug？

Worker 修复的是图表生成脚本 `gen_fig_per_model_asr.py` 中的 `_invert()` 逻辑。但如果主实验管线（`run_plan001_step1_api_scoring.py` → 分析管线）中也用了相同的 position bias ASR 计算逻辑，那么：

1. **plan001_step1_full_v4 的所有 position_bias condition 结果都需要重新计算**
2. OLS/WCB/meta-analytic pooling 中 2/12 conditions（MMLU×position_bias, ARC×position_bias）会变化
3. η_max 和 K_eff 的 β_std 可能变化
4. 论文正文中引用的所有数字可能需要更新

**之前的 ASR violin plot 也显示 Position Bias 是最强攻击（~0.49）**，如果那也是 buggy 数据，整个攻击类型排序会改变。

**请立即让 Worker 检查主分析管线中 position bias ASR 的计算逻辑**，确认是否与图表脚本使用相同的（有 bug 的）方法。如果是，这将影响整篇论文的核心数据。

---

[Reviewer 回复]:
[severity: critical] fig_keff_eta_scatter K=5 版本 — Pool A 定义错误，r=0.85 vs caption 的 r=0.50

Worker 用 9 个 API 模型定义 Pool A，得到 r=0.85。但 appendix caption 和 analysis.tex 中的 Pool A 是 **capability-matched 子集**（r=0.50, 126 panels），不是全部 API 模型。

**当前图**: Full pool r=0.81 ✓, Pool A r=**0.85** ← 与 caption r=**0.50** 严重不匹配

**根因**: Pool A 的原始定义是通过 capability 匹配筛选的面板子集（使 K_eff 和 η_max 部分解耦），不是简单的"只用 API 模型组成的面板"。Worker 使用了错误的 Pool A 定义。

**另外**: 此图覆盖了之前的 K=3 版本。threat_model.tex:6 仍引用同一文件但 caption 描述 K=3。目前图是 K=5 数据 — threat_model.tex 的引用再次不匹配。

**建议**：
1. 找到 Pool A 的原始定义（应在主分析脚本中），用正确定义重画
2. 拆分为 K=3 和 K=5 两个独立图文件，分别服务 threat_model.tex 和 appendix.tex

```text
This is very serious. Two new critical findings:

1. **Position Bias ASR calculation bug**: The position bias attack ASR was calculated incorrectly (inverting all scores instead of only gt=A swapped ones). This would mean position_bias ASR was artificially inflated from 0-3% to 43-55% for top models. If this bug exists in the main analysis pipeline, then ALL position bias conditions (2/12) in the paper have wrong data, affecting OLS, bootstrap, FDR counts, etc.

2. **Pool A definition error**: Worker used "API-only models" as Pool A instead of the capability-matched subset. 

The Pool A definition should target a subset where the correlation between K_eff and η_max is around 0.50, not the 0.85 we're currently seeing. There's also a note from user 曹晓玉 about ensuring we're using the correct data — likely referring to the underlying datasets rather than just citations.

The position bias calculation bug is the most critical issue here. If it's baked into the main pipeline, this could fundamentally compromise the paper's findings. I need to respond to the user about the current status, urgently have the agent verify the position bias calculation in the main pipeline, and then address the Pool A definition problem. I can't interrupt the Agent's current task, but I should prioritize the position bias bug verification by using an urgent message to push it to the front of the queue.
```

259	\paragraph{API vs.\ Local Model Vulnerability.} The 9 API models span a 16$\times$ vulnerability range ($\bar{\eta}$: 0.003--0.363 on ARC). The 6 locally-hosted AWQ-quantized models (Qwen2.5-72B/32B/14B, LLaMA3.1-70B/8B, Mistral-Large) were evaluated on the remote server under identical attack conditions; their aggregate vulnerability exceeds API models by approximately 2$\times$ on MMLU and 1.5$\times$ on ARC. The quantization dummy analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:quant_dummy}) confirms that part of $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s signal reflects this API--local capability gap rather than independence per se, though the effect attenuates after controlling for quantization status.

   Earlier requests (from prior context) included visual changes to Figures 2, 4, and 5, filling page 8 blank space, and fixing annotation overlaps.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - BH (Benjamini-Hochberg) FDR correction for multiple testing across 12 conditions
   - Wild cluster bootstrap with Rademacher weights, max-p aggregation, 3-position clustering
   - Log-log OLS regression: log(ASR+ε) ~ log(K_eff) + log(η_max+ε), ε=10⁻⁶
   - Meta-analytic pooling of standardized β coefficients across conditions
   - Panel enumeration: C(15,3)=455, C(15,5)=3003, C(15,7)=6435
   - K_eff (effective independence from pairwise MI, Eq. 1 in paper)
   - η_max (weakest-link vulnerability = max per-judge failure probability)
   - Error-as-tie bug: `.get('winner', 'tie')` treated API errors as ties, inflating η_max effects
   - Position bias ASR inversion bug: `_invert()` applied to all samples instead of only gt=A swapped ones
   - Stochastic dominance analysis with tier-based breakdown
   - Four aggregation methods: majority vote, weighted vote, threshold (unanimous), Bayesian calibration
   - Fractional logit with clustered SE as robustness check
   - Monte Carlo calibration of wild cluster bootstrap (1000 permutations, max null rejection 1.6%)
   - Pool A: capability-matched subsample (r≈0.50 between K_eff and η_max), NOT simply API-only models

3. Files and Code Sections:

   - **`docs/paper/main.tex`** (line 1-54): Central paper file with abstract containing key numerical claims (β_std 0.264→0.102→0.084, r=0.70–0.81, R² threshold 0.83/Bayesian 0.09, Monte Carlo 1.6%). Previously edited to remove `\clearpage` before Limitations.

22	\title{The Diminishing Weakest Link: How Panel Size Shifts\\Adversarial Vulnerability Drivers in LLM Judge Panels}
23	
24	\author{Anonymous}
25	
26	\begin{document}
27	\maketitle
28	
29	\begin{abstract}
30	Multi-judge panels are widely used to defend Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation against manipulation, yet no existing work disentangles whether robustness gains stem from individual judge quality or inter-judge diversity. We decompose panel attack success rate into weakest-link susceptibility ($\eta_{\max}$) and effective independence ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$) across 15 models, six attack types, two multiple-choice benchmarks, and panel sizes $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$ (up to 6,435 panels). Two findings are robust across diversity metrics and inference methods: (1)~diversity is uniformly positively associated with vulnerability (both $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\kappa$-diversity confirm positive direction in 12/12 conditions), and (2)~$\eta_{\max}$'s predictive power diminishes monotonically with panel size (meta-analytic pooled $\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}$: $0.264 \to 0.102 \to 0.084$; confidence interval crosses zero at $K{\geq}5$). Monte Carlo calibration confirms the wild cluster bootstrap is conservative (max null rejection $1.6\%$ at $\alpha{=}5\%$). Aggregation rules modulate predictability ($R^2$: threshold 0.83, Bayesian 0.09). The diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) precludes causal claims; all findings are associational\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/cert_manip_resist_eval-588E/}.}.
31	\end{abstract}
32	
33	\input{introduction}
34	\input{related_work}
35	\input{threat_model}
36	\input{methodology}
37	\input{results}
38	\input{analysis}
39	\input{conclusion}
40	
41	\section*{Limitations}
42	
43	\textbf{Diversity--capability confound.} The structural coupling between diversity and capability ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; VIF $\approx 2.0$--$2.9$) prevents observational regression from reliably separating their contributions; capability-matched subsampling reduces but does not eliminate this confound (Pool~A $r{=}0.50$). Causal identification requires training-based diversity control.
44	\textbf{Inference and measurement sensitivity.} At $K{=}3$, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s significance is method-sensitive (OLS 12/12, bootstrap 4/12, fractional logit 9/12), though direction is positive in all 12 conditions. Cluster counts fall below the ${\geq}20$ threshold at all panel sizes \citep{cameron2008bootstrap}; the diversity significance trajectory is also operationalization-dependent ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ vs.\ $\kappa$-diversity show opposite $K$-trends).
45	\textbf{Benchmark and attack scope.} Both benchmarks use multiple-choice formats; open-ended generation may present different attack surfaces. The six local models use AWQ INT4 quantization, which may confound capability classification, though excluding near-chance quantized models strengthens the association (Appendix~\ref{app:near_chance}). Each attack uses 1--2 template variants, limiting generalization. We study only odd $K \in \{3,5,7\}$; the predictor shift threshold $K^*$ is not precisely identified.
46	\textbf{Aggregation scope.} We examine only majority vote, threshold, and Bayesian calibration as aggregation rules; more sophisticated strategies (e.g., weighted voting, learned aggregation, or iterative deliberation among judges) may alter the diversity--robustness relationship and are not explored here.
47	\textbf{Temporal snapshot.} All API model evaluations were collected during a fixed time window; providers may update model weights or safety filters without notice, potentially altering individual susceptibility profiles and panel-level robustness patterns.
48	
49	\bibliography{references}
50	
51	\input{appendix}
52	\input{appendix_attacks}
53	
54	\end{document}

Two $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ exceptions at $K{=}3$ (score manipulation and authority bias on MMLU) show near-zero coefficients ($\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}{<}0.04$), indicating that these attacks target individual judge capability rather than exploiting inter-judge independence.}

```text
1	\section{Analysis}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound.}
5	$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ correlate at $r{=}0.70$--$0.81$ (VIF${\approx}2.0$--$2.9$; Figure~\ref{fig:keff_eta_scatter}, Appendix~\ref{app:extended_analysis}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis \citep{cinelli2020making} shows the confound could account for the association in 69\% of conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}), though fractional logit confirms the positive association (9/12 at all $K$). Causal attribution requires capability-controlled designs (Appendices~\ref{app:sensitivity_poolA},~\ref{app:cross_benchmark}).
6	
7	\paragraph{API-Only vs.\ Full Pool.}
8	Restricting to 9 API models removes the AWQ confound but increases collinearity ($r{=}0.85$): $\eta_{\max}$ retains significance (8/12) while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ drops (2--3/12); the API-only pool lacks statistical power (MDE $> 0.9$; Appendices~\ref{app:quant_dummy},~\ref{app:random_subsample}).
9	
10	\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}
11	Aggregation rules influence predictability but not $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction (Figure~\ref{fig:aggregation_heatmap}). Threshold voting yields the highest $R^2$ ($0.83$ at $K{=}3$), while Bayesian calibration nearly neutralizes panel composition effects ($R^2{\leq}0.17$ across all $K$). At $K{=}7$, Bayesian calibration fully neutralizes $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (0/12; Appendix~\ref{app:bayesian_entropy}). Robustness-first selection ($\min \eta_{\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in all 12 conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:panel_selection}), confirming that selecting judges by individual robustness dominates diversity-maximizing strategies regardless of panel size. Clean $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is stable under 4/6 attacks ($\rho{\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\ref{app:attacked_keff}), offering a deployable metric from clean-condition agreement alone.
12	
13	\paragraph{Metric Sensitivity of the Diversity Trajectory.}
14	The diversity significance trajectory is operationalization-dependent: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance increases ($4 \to 7 \to 8$/12) while $\kappa$-diversity \citep{kuncheva2003measures} decreases ($8 \to 7 \to 6$/12; Appendix~\ref{app:kappa_regime}). Shannon MI entropy shows a third pattern ($4 \to 4 \to 0$; Appendix~\ref{app:shannon_entropy}). Both $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\kappa$-diversity confirm positive direction (12/12), but the $K$-dependent trajectory is metric-specific; the $\eta_{\max}$ decline holds under all specifications, making it the more robust finding across operationalizations.
15	
```

Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis \\citep{cinelli2020making} shows the confound could account for the association in 69\\% of conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}), though fractional logit confirms the positive association (9/12 at all $K$). Causal attribution requires capability-controlled designs (Appendices~\\ref{app:sensitivity_poolA},~\\ref{app:cross_benchmark}).\n\n\\paragraph{API-Only vs.\\ Full Pool.}\nRestricting to 9 API models removes the AWQ confound but increases collinearity ($r{=}0.85$): $\\eta_{\\max}$ retains significance (8/12) while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ drops (2--3/12); the API-only pool lacks statistical power (MDE $> 0.9$; Appendices~\\ref{app:quant_dummy},~\\ref{app:random_subsample}).\n\n\\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}\nAggregation rules influence predictability but not $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction (Figure~\\ref{fig:aggregation_heatmap}). Threshold voting yields the highest $R^2$ ($0.83$ at $K{=}3$), while Bayesian calibration nearly neutralizes panel composition effects ($R^2{\\leq}0.17$ across all $K$). At $K{=}7$, Bayesian calibration fully neutralizes $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (0/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:bayesian_entropy}). Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in all 12 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:panel_selection}), confirming that selecting judges by individual robustness dominates diversity-maximizing strategies regardless of panel size. Clean $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is stable under 4/6 attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}), offering a deployable metric from clean-condition agreement alone.\n\n\\paragraph{Metric Sensitivity of the Diversity Trajectory.}\nThe diversity significance trajectory is operationalization-dependent: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance increases ($4 \\to 7 \\to 8$/12) while $\\kappa$-diversity \\citep{kuncheva2003measures} decreases ($8 \\to 7 \\to 6$/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:kappa_regime}). Shannon MI entropy shows a third pattern ($4 \\to 4 \\to 0$; Appendix~\\ref{app:shannon_entropy}). Both $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity confirm positive direction (12/12), but the $K$-dependent trajectory is metric-specific; the $\\eta_{\\max}$ decline holds under all specifications, making it the more robust finding across operationalizations.\n",

980	\log(\mathrm{ASR} + \epsilon) = \alpha + \beta_1 \log(K_{\mathrm{eff}}) + \beta_2 \log(\eta_{\max} + \epsilon) + \beta_3 D_{\mathrm{quant}}.
981	\end{equation}
982	Adding the dummy attenuates $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s standardized coefficient from $+0.39$ ($K{=}3$) to $+0.34$ ($K{=}5$) to $+0.27$ ($K{=}7$), with FDR-significant counts of 6/12, 6/12, and 4/12 respectively. The attenuation confirms partial confounding: some of $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s explanatory power reflects the capability gap between quantized and API models rather than independence per se. However, collinearity between $D_{\mathrm{quant}}$ and both predictors escalates sharply with panel size (VIF${=}162$ at $K{=}7$), rendering the dummy unreliable for large panels. The quantization dummy analysis is therefore informative at $K{=}3$ (moderate VIF) but should be interpreted cautiously at $K{\geq}5$.
983	
984	\section{Bayesian Posterior Concentration Analysis}
985	\label{app:bayesian_entropy}
986	
987	Bayesian calibration neutralizes $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}7$ (0/12 significant; \S\ref{sec:analysis}). One hypothesized explanation is that the posterior concentrates on 2--3 accurate judges, compressing effective panel diversity. We test this by computing the posterior concentration ratio $C = \max_j w_j^{\mathrm{post}} / \min_j w_j^{\mathrm{post}}$ across panels.
988	
989	The concentration ratio declines modestly across panel sizes: $C = 0.890$ ($K{=}3$), $0.874$ ($K{=}5$), $0.867$ ($K{=}7$), a total change of only 2.3 percentage points. This gradual decline is insufficient to explain the abrupt neutralization at $K{=}7$ (from 6/12 at $K{=}3$ to 0/12 at $K{=}7$). The pattern is therefore not explained by simple posterior concentration; rather, the interaction between Bayesian weighting and the combinatorial structure of larger panels likely produces the neutralization. Full characterization of this interaction remains an open question.
990	
991	\section{Monte Carlo Calibration of Wild Cluster Bootstrap}
992	\label{app:monte_carlo}
993	
994	To verify that the wild cluster bootstrap does not over-reject under the null hypothesis, we conduct a Monte Carlo calibration study. For each of the 36 conditions (12 attack$\times$benchmark conditions $\times$ 3 panel sizes), we generate 1{,}000 permutation datasets by randomly shuffling the ASR outcome within each cluster, destroying any true association between predictors and ASR. For each permuted dataset, we run the wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}999$, Rademacher weights) and record whether the null is rejected at $\alpha{=}0.05$.
995	
996	Across all 36 conditions, the maximum empirical null rejection rate is 1.6\%, well below the nominal $\alpha{=}5\%$. The median rejection rate is 0.4\%. This confirms that the wild cluster bootstrap is conservative in our combinatorial design: the reported significance counts are lower bounds on what a correctly calibrated test would yield. The conservatism is expected given the low cluster counts (13, ${\leq}11$, ${\leq}9$ position clusters at $K{=}3,5,7$) and the use of Rademacher weights \citep{mackinnon2018wild}.
997	
998	\begin{figure}[h]
999	\centering
1000	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_bootstrap_null.pdf}
1001	\caption{Empirical null rejection rates across 36 conditions ($12 \times 3$ panel sizes) under 1{,}000 permutations per condition (wild cluster bootstrap, $B{=}999$, Rademacher weights). The maximum rejection rate is 1.6\% at nominal $\alpha{=}5\%$ (dashed line), confirming conservative calibration across all experimental conditions.}
1002	\label{fig:bootstrap_null}
1003	\end{figure}
1004	
1005	Figure~\ref{fig:bootstrap_null} visualizes the Monte Carlo null rejection rates. No condition exceeds the nominal 5\% threshold, with the vast majority below 1\%, providing strong evidence that the bootstrap procedure does not inflate type~I error in our combinatorial design.
1006	
1007	\subsection{Fractional Logit Specification Details}
1008	\label{app:fractional_logit_detail}
1009	
1010	The log-log OLS specification (Equation~\ref{eq:regression}) faces two methodological concerns: (1) the $\log(\mathrm{ASR} + \epsilon)$ transformation introduces sensitivity to the $\epsilon$ floor (Appendix~\ref{app:epsilon}), and (2) predicted values are unbounded, potentially falling outside $[0, 1]$. The fractional logit model \citep{papke1996econometric} addresses both by modeling ASR directly as a quasi-likelihood with logit link:
1011	\begin{equation}
1012	g(\mu_i) = \beta_0 + \beta_1 \log K_{\mathrm{eff},i} + \beta_2 \log(\eta_{\max,i} + \epsilon),
1013	\end{equation}
1014	where $g(\cdot) = \log(\cdot/(1-\cdot))$ is the logit link, $\mu_i = E[\mathrm{ASR}_i]$, and the quasi-likelihood variance is $V(\mu_i) = \mu_i(1-\mu_i)$. Inference uses the Bernoulli quasi-likelihood with clustered standard errors (same max-$p$ aggregation as the bootstrap).
1015	
1016	Table~\ref{tab:fraclogit_detail} compares OLS and fractional logit coefficient directions across all 72 condition$\times K$ combinations.
1017	
1018	\begin{table}[h]
1019	\centering
1020	\small
1021	\begin{tabular}{rcccc}
1022	\toprule
1023	$\boldsymbol{K}$ & \textbf{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ concordance} & \textbf{$\eta_{\max}$ concordance} & \textbf{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ sig (FL)} & \textbf{$\eta_{\max}$ sig (FL)} \\
1024	\midrule
1025	3 & 12/12 & 12/12 & 9/12 & 7/12 \\
1026	5 & 12/12 & 11/12 & 9/12 & 5/12 \\
1027	7 & 12/12 & 11/12 & 9/12 & 4/12 \\
1028	\midrule
1029	Total & 36/36 & 34/36 & 27/36 & 16/36 \\
1030	\bottomrule
1031	\end{tabular}
1032	\caption{Fractional logit vs.\ OLS comparison across $K$ values. Concordance: fraction of conditions where the coefficient direction (positive/negative) matches between OLS and fractional logit. $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ direction is perfectly concordant (36/36); $\eta_{\max}$ shows 2 discordances (both at high $K$ where the coefficient is near zero). Fractional logit significance is computed with clustered SE (max-$p$, BH FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$).}
1033	\label{tab:fraclogit_detail}
1034	\end{table}
1035	
1036	The 98.6\% overall directional concordance (71/72) confirms that the main OLS findings are not artifacts of the log transformation. The two discordances occur for $\eta_{\max}$ in ARC$\times$score manipulation at $K{=}5$ and $K{=}7$, where both OLS and fractional logit coefficients are near zero ($|\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}|{<}0.05$) and the sign flip is within sampling noise. $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ achieves perfect concordance (36/36), and its fractional logit significance (9/12 at every $K$) is stable across panel sizes---notably higher than the wild cluster bootstrap (4/12, 7/12, 8/12), reflecting the fractional logit's greater efficiency for bounded outcomes.

      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/appendix.tex",
      "content": "\\log(\\mathrm{ASR} + \\epsilon) = \\alpha + \\beta_1 \\log(K_{\\mathrm{eff}}) + \\beta_2 \\log(\\eta_{\\max} + \\epsilon) + \\beta_3 D_{\\mathrm{quant}}.\n\\end{equation}\nAdding the dummy attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s standardized coefficient from $+0.39$ ($K{=}3$) to $+0.34$ ($K{=}5$) to $+0.27$ ($K{=}7$), with FDR-significant counts of 6/12, 6/12, and 4/12 respectively. The attenuation confirms partial confounding: some of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s explanatory power reflects the capability gap between quantized and API models rather than independence per se. However, collinearity between $D_{\\mathrm{quant}}$ and both predictors escalates sharply with panel size (VIF${=}162$ at $K{=}7$), rendering the dummy unreliable for large panels. The quantization dummy analysis is therefore informative at $K{=}3$ (moderate VIF) but should be interpreted cautiously at $K{\\geq}5$.\n\n\\section{Bayesian Posterior Concentration Analysis}\n\\label{app:bayesian_entropy}\n\nBayesian calibration neutralizes $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}7$ (0/12 significant; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). One hypothesized explanation is that the posterior concentrates on 2--3 accurate judges, compressing effective panel diversity. We test this by computing the posterior concentration ratio $C = \\max_j w_j^{\\mathrm{post}} / \\min_j w_j^{\\mathrm{post}}$ across panels.\n\nThe concentration ratio declines modestly across panel sizes: $C = 0.890$ ($K{=}3$), $0.874$ ($K{=}5$), $0.867$ ($K{=}7$), a total change of only 2.3 percentage points. This gradual decline is insufficient to explain the abrupt neutralization at $K{=}7$ (from 6/12 at $K{=}3$ to 0/12 at $K{=}7$). The pattern is therefore not explained by simple posterior concentration; rather, the interaction between Bayesian weighting and the combinatorial structure of larger panels likely produces the neutralization. Full characterization of this interaction remains an open question.\n\n\\section{Monte Carlo Calibration of Wild Cluster Bootstrap}\n\\label{app:monte_carlo}\n\nTo verify that the wild cluster bootstrap does not over-reject under the null hypothesis, we conduct a Monte Carlo calibration study. For each of the 36 conditions (12 attack$\\times$benchmark conditions $\\times$ 3 panel sizes), we generate 1{,}000 permutation datasets by randomly shuffling the ASR outcome within each cluster, destroying any true association between predictors and ASR. For each permuted dataset, we run the wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}999$, Rademacher weights) and record whether the null is rejected at $\\alpha{=}0.05$.\n\nAcross all 36 conditions, the maximum empirical null rejection rate is 1.6\\%, well below the nominal $\\alpha{=}5\\%$. The median rejection rate is 0.4\\%. This confirms that the wild cluster bootstrap is conservative in our combinatorial design: the reported significance counts are lower bounds on what a correctly calibrated test would yield. The conservatism is expected given the low cluster counts (13, ${\\leq}11$, ${\\leq}9$ position clusters at $K{=}3,5,7$) and the use of Rademacher weights \\citep{mackinnon2018wild}.\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_bootstrap_null.pdf}\n\\caption{Empirical null rejection rates across 36 conditions ($12 \\times 3$ panel sizes) under 1{,}000 permutations per condition (wild cluster bootstrap, $B{=}999$, Rademacher weights). The maximum rejection rate is 1.6\\% at nominal $\\alpha{=}5\\%$ (dashed line), confirming conservative calibration across all experimental conditions.}\n\\label{fig:bootstrap_null}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:bootstrap_null} visualizes the Monte Carlo null rejection rates. No condition exceeds the nominal 5\\% threshold, with the vast majority below 1\\%, providing strong evidence that the bootstrap procedure does not inflate type~I error in our combinatorial design.\n\n\\subsection{Fractional Logit Specification Details}\n\\label{app:fractional_logit_detail}\n\nThe log-log OLS specification (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) faces two methodological concerns: (1) the $\\log(\\mathrm{ASR} + \\epsilon)$ transformation introduces sensitivity to the $\\epsilon$ floor (Appendix~\\ref{app:epsilon}), and (2) predicted values are unbounded, potentially falling outside $[0, 1]$. The fractional logit model \\citep{papke1996econometric} addresses both by modeling ASR directly as a quasi-likelihood with logit link:\n\\begin{equation}\ng(\\mu_i) = \\beta_0 + \\beta_1 \\log K_{\\mathrm{eff},i} + \\beta_2 \\log(\\eta_{\\max,i} + \\epsilon),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $g(\\cdot) = \\log(\\cdot/(1-\\cdot))$ is the logit link, $\\mu_i = E[\\mathrm{ASR}_i]$, and the quasi-likelihood variance is $V(\\mu_i) = \\mu_i(1-\\mu_i)$. Inference uses the Bernoulli quasi-likelihood with clustered standard errors (same max-$p$ aggregation as the bootstrap).\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:fraclogit_detail} compares OLS and fractional logit coefficient directions across all 72 condition$\\times K$ combinations.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rcccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\boldsymbol{K}$ & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ concordance} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ concordance} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig (FL)} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig (FL)} \\\\\n\\midrule\n3 & 12/12 & 12/12 & 9/12 & 7/12 \\\\\n5 & 12/12 & 11/12 & 9/12 & 5/12 \\\\\n7 & 12/12 & 11/12 & 9/12 & 4/12 \\\\\n\\midrule\nTotal & 36/36 & 34/36 & 27/36 & 16/36 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Fractional logit vs.\\ OLS comparison across $K$ values. Concordance: fraction of conditions where the coefficient direction (positive/negative) matches between OLS and fractional logit. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction is perfectly concordant (36/36); $\\eta_{\\max}$ shows 2 discordances (both at high $K$ where the coefficient is near zero). Fractional logit significance is computed with clustered SE (max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$).}\n\\label{tab:fraclogit_detail}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe 98.6\\% overall directional concordance (71/72) confirms that the main OLS findings are not artifacts of the log transformation. The two discordances occur for $\\eta_{\\max}$ in ARC$\\times$score manipulation at $K{=}5$ and $K{=}7$, where both OLS and fractional logit coefficients are near zero ($|\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}|{<}0.05$) and the sign flip is within sampling noise. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ achieves perfect concordance (36/36), and its fractional logit significance (9/12 at every $K$) is stable across panel sizes---notably higher than the wild cluster bootstrap (4/12, 7/12, 8/12), reflecting the fractional logit's greater efficiency for bounded outcomes.\n\n\\subsection{Attack Taxonomy and Effect Size Summary}\n\\label{app:attack_taxonomy}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:attack_effect_sizes} provides a comprehensive summary of per-attack effect sizes and classification, synthesizing results across the main text and appendices.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{2.5pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccccccc}\n\\toprule\n& & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{$\\boldsymbol{K{=}3}$} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{$\\boldsymbol{K{=}5}$} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{$\\boldsymbol{K{=}7}$} & & \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){3-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-6} \\cmidrule(lr){7-8}\n\\textbf{Attack} & \\textbf{Type} & $\\bar{\\beta}_{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ & $\\bar{\\beta}_{\\eta}$ & $\\bar{\\beta}_{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ & $\\bar{\\beta}_{\\eta}$ & $\\bar{\\beta}_{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ & $\\bar{\\beta}_{\\eta}$ & \\textbf{MI stab.} & \\textbf{Target} \\\\\n\\midrule\nPrompt inj. & Targeted & 0.291 & 0.430 & 0.538 & 0.166 & 0.593 & 0.099 & Moderate & Instructions \\\\\nSycophancy & Shared & $-0.013$ & 0.489 & 0.082 & 0.362 & 0.077 & 0.319 & High & Preference \\\\\nScore manip. & Targeted & 0.143 & 0.411 & 0.475 & 0.025 & 0.461 & $-0.015$ & High & Scoring \\\\\nVerbosity & Shared & 0.118 & 0.468 & 0.225 & 0.361 & 0.229 & 0.337 & High & Length \\\\\nPosition & Structural & 0.344 & 0.204 & 0.515 & $-0.008$ & 0.531 & $-0.024$ & Low & Order ```text
Let me check the VIF data. The paper says VIF ≈ 2.0–2.9. Looking at the data:
- Mean VIF = 2.978
- Min VIF = 1.235
- Max VIF = 7.027

So the range 2.0-2.9 doesn't match the data. The mean is 2.98, and per-condition values range from 1.23 to 7.03. If we look at the 6 MMLU conditions:


- MMLU prompt_injection: 1.99
- MMLU sycophancy: 1.23
- MMLU score_manipulation: 3.02
- MMLU verbosity_bias: 1.25
- MMLU position_bias: 1.78
- MMLU authority_bias: 1.63

The ARC conditions are much higher, with score_manipulation hitting 7.03 and position_bias at 6.53, while the others cluster around 2-3.

Looking at all these VIF values together, they range from 1.23 to 7.03, but the paper's claim of "2.0–2.9" doesn't match that spread. Let me sort through these to figure out what statistic they're actually reporting — it looks like the median falls around 2.35, with the middle 50% of values between roughly 1.7 and 3.3.

Now I'm realizing the paper might be referencing different K values entirely. If the correlation between K_eff and η_max varies from 0.70 to 0.81 across different configurations, I can work backwards using the VIF formula to see if that explains the 2.0–2.9 range they cite — and it does check out mathematically. I'm checking which K values correspond to those correlation coefficients—K=3 gives r=0.70, and I need to verify whether K=5 or K=7 produces r=0.81. The paper cites these values with a reference to a scatter plot and an appendix figure, so I should look at the actual data files or bootstrap summaries to confirm the exact correlations for each K value. Then I can move on to examining the Cinelli-Hazlett 69% claim.

_source line 690 · 2026-05-25T20:27:06.489Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Let me analyze the Cinelli-Hazlett sensitivity analysis results:

The paper says "Cinelli–Hazlett sensitivity analysis shows the confound could account for the association in 69% of conditions". The summary shows `rv_exceeds_eta_count: 8/36`.

If RV exceeds η's benchmarks in 8/36 conditions, that means K_eff is robust to confounding in 8/36 conditions. The confound could account for the association in 28/36 = 77.8% of conditions, not 69%.

Wait, but maybe the 69% is about something else. 

Looking back at the source, I need to check if the 69% figure refers to a specific subset of the data. Breaking down by K values: at K=3, only 1 out of 12 conditions show robustness, meaning the confound could account for 91.7% of cases; at K=5, it's 3 out of 12 showing robustness, so 75% of conditions are vulnerable; and at K=7, the pattern continues with 4 out of 12 robust. The 69% might be a weighted average or calculated differently than my initial interpretation. I'm looking at the breakdown by K level to find where the 69% figure comes from. The percentages vary across different K values—K=3 shows 91.7%, K=5 shows 75%, and K=7 shows 8—so maybe the paper is reporting a specific subset or averaging across certain conditions rather than all 36. I'm wondering if the 69% figure might be measuring something different—perhaps the proportion of conditions where the adjusted estimate would flip to zero or negative after accounting for confounding. Let me check the sensitivity analysis details in the appendix to clarify what's actually being reported.
```

Now I need to check the 69% Cinelli-Hazlett claim in the appendix.

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_013kaRWr6WZeM1yE6fcKg6qz`</summary>

```text
528:Section~\ref{sec:cross_k} reports pooled effect sizes using DerSimonian--Laird (DL) random effects \citep{dersimonian1986meta}. Here we detail the pooling procedure and assess sensitivity to alternative meta-analytic methods.
640:\section{Cinelli--Hazlett Sensitivity Analysis}
641:\label{app:sensitivity}
643:We apply the Cinelli--Hazlett omitted variable bias framework to quantify how robust $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is to unobserved confounders. For each of the 36 conditions ($3K \times 2$ benchmarks $\times$ $6$ attacks), we compute the robustness value (RV): the minimum strength an unobserved confounder must have, in terms of partial $R^2$ with both treatment and outcome, to reduce $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient to zero.
696:[Omitted long matching line]
697:\label{tab:sensitivity}
704:\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig_sensitivity_contour_K3.pdf}
705:\caption{Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity contour plots at $K{=}3$ (12 conditions). Each panel shows the adjusted $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient as a function of hypothetical confounder strength (partial $R^2$ with treatment and outcome). Diamonds mark the $\eta_{\max}$ confound benchmark. At $K{=}3$, the benchmark falls outside the zero-contour in 4/12 conditions, indicating partial robustness.}
706:\label{fig:sensitivity_contour_K3}
711:\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig_sensitivity_contour_K5.pdf}
712:\caption{Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity contour plots at $K{=}5$ (12 conditions). Format identical to Figure~\ref{fig:sensitivity_contour_K3}. The $\eta_{\max}$ benchmark falls inside the zero-contour for 9/12 conditions, indicating that the known capability confound could explain away $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s effect in the majority of conditions at this panel size.}
713:\label{fig:sensitivity_contour}
718:\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig_sensitivity_contour_K7.pdf}
719:\caption{Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity contour plots at $K{=}7$ (12 conditions). Format identical to Figure~\ref{fig:sensitivity_contour_K3}. RV heterogeneity is most pronounced at this panel size: MMLU prompt injection achieves RV${=}0.52$ while MMLU authority bias drops to RV${=}0.005$ (Table~\ref{tab:sensitivity}).}
720:\label{fig:sensitivity_contour_K7}
737:\section{Pool A Cinelli--Hazlett Sensitivity}
738:\label{app:sensitivity_poolA}
740:To test whether reducing the $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$--$\eta_{\max}$ collinearity strengthens $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s independent signal, we repeat the Cinelli--Hazlett analysis on the capability-matched Pool~A subsample (9 models, $r{=}0.50$, 84 panels at $K{=}3$, 126 at $K{=}5$). Table~\ref{tab:sensitivity_poolA} compares the full-pool results with Pool~A.
755:\caption{Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity comparison: full pool vs.\ capability-matched Pool~A. RV $>$ $\eta$: conditions where $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s robustness value exceeds the known $\eta_{\max}$ confound benchmark. Pool~A's reduced collinearity ($r{=}0.50$ vs.\ $0.81$ at $K{=}5$) increases the fraction of robust conditions from 1/12 to 2/12 at $K{=}3$ and from 3/12 to 10/12 at $K{=}5$, with the largest gains at $K{=}5$ where both benchmarks improve equally (ARC 5/6, MMLU 5/6).}
756:\label{tab:sensitivity_poolA}
781:[Omitted long matching line]
786:[Omitted long matching line]
869:[Omitted long matching line]
1010:The log-log OLS specification (Equation~\ref{eq:regression}) faces two methodological concerns: (1) the $\log(\mathrm{ASR} + \epsilon)$ transformation introduces sensitivity to the $\epsilon$ floor (Appendix~\ref{app:epsilon}), and (2) predicted values are unbounded, potentially falling outside $[0, 1]$. The fractional logit model \citep{papke1996econometric} addresses both by modeling ASR directly as a quasi-likelihood with logit link:
1085:\caption{Near-chance model sensitivity: FDR-significant $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ conditions out of 12 (2 benchmarks $\times$ 6 attacks). $\bar{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}$: mean standardized $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient across conditions. The full-pool fractional logit count at $K{=}3$ (8/12) differs by $\pm 1$ from Table~\ref{tab:method_comparison} (9/12) due to clustering implementation details in the sensitivity script; this does not affect within-table comparisons.}

15	
16	These findings yield three contributions:
17	\begin{enumerate}
18	\item \textbf{Diversity uniformly positively associated with vulnerability.} Both $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\kappa$-diversity \citep{kuncheva2003measures} confirm a positive association with panel attack success in all 12 conditions (12/12 direction under OLS and generalized additive model bootstrap). However, the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) precludes causal interpretation: Cinelli--Hazlett robustness values do not exceed the confound in 69\% of conditions (\S\ref{sec:dominance}, \S\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).
19	\item \textbf{Diminishing weakest link.} $\eta_{\max}$'s predictive power declines monotonically with panel size: meta-analytic pooled $\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}$ falls from $0.264$ [$0.158, 0.369$] at $K{=}3$ to $0.102$ [$-0.055, 0.259$] at $K{=}5$ to $0.084$ [$-0.083, 0.251$] at $K{=}7$; the confidence interval crosses zero at $K{\geq}5$. The diversity significance trajectory, however, depends on the metric: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance increases ($4 \to 7 \to 8$/12) while $\kappa$-diversity decreases ($8 \to 7 \to 6$/12), indicating that the $K$-dependent pattern is operationalization-sensitive (\S\ref{sec:cross_k}).
20	\item \textbf{Aggregation as predictability control.} Aggregation rules modulate how much variance panel composition explains ($R^2$: threshold 0.83, Bayesian 0.09). Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\eta_{\max}$ persists (9/12), so the defense is partial (\S\ref{sec:aggregation}, \S\ref{sec:analysis}).
21	\end{enumerate}
22	
23	All findings are associational; the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; \S\ref{sec:analysis}) precludes causal claims.
24

```text
Let me trace the "69%" claim more carefully.

The paper says: "Cinelli--Hazlett robustness values do not exceed the confound in 69% of conditions".

From the data: rv_exceeds_eta_count = 8/36, meaning RV exceeds η benchmark in 8/36 = 22.2% of conditions. So RV does NOT exceed in 28/36 = 77.8% of conditions.

But the paper says 69%. So 69% ≠ 77.8%. This is a discrepancy!
 
Now I'm wondering if the 69% refers to a specific subset—maybe just the K=3 conditions, or perhaps the data was computed before a bug fix. I should check if there are multiple sensitivity analysis files with different versions of the data.

_source line 729 · 2026-05-25T20:30:30.473Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Let me verify the κ-diversity trajectory claims in the paper:

Paper says (analysis.tex line 14): "K_eff significance increases (4 → 7 → 8/12) while κ-diversity decreases (8 → 7 → 6/12)"

From the kappa_diversity_regime_transition.json:
- K3: kappa_div_fdr_sig = 8/12 ✓


- K5: kappa_div_fdr_sig = 7/12 ✓
- K7: kappa_div_fdr_sig = 6/12 ✓

The κ-diversity trajectory checks out. But when I look at the K_eff values in the comparison_with_keff section, K3 matches at 4/12, but K5 shows 10/12 in the data while the paper claims 7/12 — that's a discrepancy I need to investigate.

Actually, I'm realizing the corrected data file has updated K_eff counts that differ from what's in the appendix tables. The comparison_with_keff section is pulling from the corrected regime_transition_corrected.json, which has K5 at 10/12 and K7 at 9/12 for K_eff significance, not the older values. So the κ-diversity metrics were computed alongside these corrected K_eff numbers, which means I need to check whether the paper's appendix reflects the original or corrected values. I'm tracking which data file corresponds to each K value and whether error corrections were applied, then cross-checking against what's currently in the paper appendix—the K=3 and K=5 K_eff values match their source files, but the η values are inconsistent. The K=7 η_max count of 1/12 in the paper also doesn't match any source—both corrected and uncorrected data show 0/12. Looking at the full K_eff trajectory, the paper reports 4→7→8/12, but only the first two values are traceable to data; the 8/12 for K=7 appears fabricated. With corrected data, this trajectory would actually be much stronger at 4→10→9/12, while uncorrected data would show 4→7→5/12. The uncorrected data shows 4→7→5, which contradicts the "increases" narrative entirely—it goes up then down. The paper's reported 4→7→8 trajectory creates a false impression of monotonic growth that neither dataset actually supports. This is a critical discrepancy because if K_eff truly decreases at K=7 in the uncorrected version, it undermines the claimed opposite trend with kappa, which itself decreases across the same range. I need to verify the API-only pool analysis next to see if that claim about removing the AWQ confound holds up. The corrected K=3 value should be around 0.180, not 0.266, which confirms this is part of the M022 discrepancy I've already identified. Now I'm ready to compile the full audit status and double-check the FDR counts in the regime transition data.
```

_source line 735 · 2026-05-25T20:31:09.126Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Let me verify the K=7 data. From the k7_wild_bootstrap_results.json, the keff_formula is "mi_based_keff (average pairwise MI)" — this is the original K_eff formula, NOT V2. I need to check what the counts are.

From the summary earlier: the k7 data file's summary field says `"keff_fdr_sig": "5/12"`, `"eta_fdr_sig": "0/12"`.

Now let me verify the corrected data's K=7 FDR counts:
From regime_transition_corrected.json K=7:


Looking at the keff_fdr_p values, I'm seeing six significant results below 0.05 across the MMLU tasks (prompt injection, sycophancy, score manipulation, verbosity bias, position bias, and authority bias), while the ARC prompt injection result at 0.0756 falls just outside the threshold.

Continuing through the ARC results, sycophancy and authority bias don't reach significance, but score manipulation, verbosity bias, and position bias all do—though the verbosity bias is right at the boundary at exactly 0.05. That gives me 9 out of 12 tests meeting the significance criterion, depending on whether I'm using strict inequality or allowing equality at the threshold.

Now I'm cross-referencing the FDR-corrected p-values, and those all stay above 0.05 across the board, so none of them register as significant by that stricter standard. Looking at the regime transition data, the K=7 configuration shows 9 significant effects, which aligns with what I'm seeing. I'm also compiling a comparison table across different data sources to see how the K_eff significance counts vary—the paper's appendix tables show 4 significant results at K=3, climbing to 7 at K=5 and 8 at K=7.

The old bootstrap data gives different counts, particularly at K=7 where I'm only seeing 5 significant results instead of the paper's 8. The corrected data with the updated formula and error filtering pushes K=5 up to 10 significant results and K=7 to 9. So the paper's K=7 count of 8 doesn't cleanly match any single data source, which suggests the appendix might be mixing different calculation methods or filtering approaches.

Looking at the η_max bootstrap FDR results, the paper reports 2/12 for K=5 and 1/12 for K=7, but both my old and corrected data sources consistently show 0/12 for both of these cases. This is a clear discrepancy. So far I've identified three specific numbers in the paper that don't align with any of my data sources: the K=5 η_max count, the K=7 K_eff count, and the K=7 η_max count.

Beyond these direct mismatches, there are several other issues queued up for correction—the K=3 appendix table β_std values that predate the bug fix, the fractional logit comparison text that references specific FDR counts which depend on which numbers turn out to be correct, and the β_std trajectory that relies on uncorrected data. I should also verify the introduction's claim about K_eff significance increasing from 4 to 7 to 8/12, since my corrected data would suggest it should be 4 to 10 to 9/12 instead. Let me check what progress the Agent has made on these items and then prepare a status update.
